<article class="ltx_document ltx_authors_1line">
<h1 class="ltx_title ltx_title_document">
It’s Hard to HAC with Average Linkage!
</h1>
<div class="ltx_authors">

<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract"><h6 class="ltx_title ltx_title_abstract">Abstract</h6>
    
<p id="abstract1.1" class="ltx_p">Average linkage Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering (HAC) is an extensively studied and applied method for hierarchical clustering. Recent applications to massive datasets have driven significant interest in near-linear-time and efficient parallel algorithms for average linkage HAC.</p>
    
<p id="abstract1.2" class="ltx_p">We provide hardness results that rule out such algorithms. On the sequential side, we establish a runtime lower bound of <math id="abstract1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n^{3/2-\epsilon}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msup><mi>n</mi><mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>/</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><mo>−</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow></msup><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n^{3/2-\epsilon}</annotation></semantics></math> on <math id="abstract1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> node graphs for sequential combinatorial algorithms under standard fine-grained complexity assumptions. This essentially matches the best-known running time for average linkage HAC. On the parallel side, we prove that average linkage HAC likely cannot be parallelized even on simple graphs by showing that it is CC-hard on trees of diameter <math id="abstract1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="4" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>4</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">4</annotation></semantics></math>. On the possibility side, we demonstrate that average linkage HAC can be efficiently parallelized (i.e., it is in NC) on paths and can be solved in near-linear time when the height of the output cluster hierarchy is small.</p>
  
</div>
<div class="ltx_pagination ltx_role_newpage"></div>
<section id="S1" class="ltx_section">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_section"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Introduction</h2>

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">Hierarchical clustering is a fundamental method for data analysis which organizes data points into a hierarchical structure so that similar points appear closer in the hierarchy. Unlike other common clustering methods, such as <math id="S1.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="k" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>k</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">k</annotation></semantics></math>-means, hierarchical clustering does not require the the number of clusters to be fixed ahead of time. This allows it to capture structures that are inherently hierarchical—such as phylogenies <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib18" title="" class="ltx_ref">18</a>]</cite> and brain structure <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib10" title="" class="ltx_ref">10</a>]</cite>.
One of the most widely used and studied methods for hierarchical clustering is Hierarchical Agglomerative Clustering (HAC) <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib23" title="" class="ltx_ref">23</a>, <a href="#bib.bib25" title="" class="ltx_ref">25</a>, <a href="#bib.bib38" title="" class="ltx_ref">38</a>]</cite>. HAC produces a hierarchy by first placing each point in its own cluster and then iteratively merging the two <em id="S1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">most similar</em> clusters until all points are aggregated into a single cluster. The similarity of two clusters is given by a <em id="S1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">linkage function</em>. HAC is included in many popular scientific computing libraries such as scikit-learn <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib36" title="" class="ltx_ref">36</a>]</cite>, SciPy <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib41" title="" class="ltx_ref">41</a>]</cite>, ALGLIB <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib37" title="" class="ltx_ref">37</a>]</cite>, Julia, R, MATLAB, Mathematica and many more <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib34" title="" class="ltx_ref">34</a>, <a href="#bib.bib35" title="" class="ltx_ref">35</a>]</cite>. This cluster hierarchy is often equivalently understood as a binary tree—a.k.a. dendrogram—whose internal nodes correspond to cluster merges.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p2.1" class="ltx_p">The proliferation of massive datasets with billions of points has driven the need for more efficient HAC algorithms that can overcome the inherent <math id="S1.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\Theta(n^{2})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Θ</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>n</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\Theta(n^{2})</annotation></semantics></math> complexity required to read all pairwise distances <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib17" title="" class="ltx_ref">17</a>, <a href="#bib.bib16" title="" class="ltx_ref">16</a>, <a href="#bib.bib30" title="" class="ltx_ref">30</a>]</cite>. Finer-grained running time bounds for HAC were recently obtained by assuming that only <math id="S1.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="m=o(n^{2})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mi>o</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>n</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m=o(n^{2})</annotation></semantics></math> pairs of points have nonzero similarity, and analyzing the running time as a function of both <math id="S1.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S1.p2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="m" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>m</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m</annotation></semantics></math>.
This is a natural assumption in practice, as in large datasets of billions of datapoints, typically a small fraction of pairs exhibit nonnegligible similarity.
In this case, the input to HAC is an edge-weighted graph, where each vertex represents an input point and each edge weight specifies the similarity between its endpoints.
This approach is convenient for large-scale applications since (1) very large clustering instances can be compactly represented as sparse weighted graphs and (2) the running time of HAC can be decoupled from the running time of nearest-neighbor search.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p3.1" class="ltx_p">A particularly common linkage function for HAC is <em id="S1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">average linkage</em>, which both optimizes reasonable global objectives <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib32" title="" class="ltx_ref">32</a>]</cite> and exhibits good empirical performance <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib28" title="" class="ltx_ref">28</a>, <a href="#bib.bib5" title="" class="ltx_ref">5</a>, <a href="#bib.bib33" title="" class="ltx_ref">33</a>, <a href="#bib.bib45" title="" class="ltx_ref">45</a>, <a href="#bib.bib21" title="" class="ltx_ref">21</a>, <a href="#bib.bib24" title="" class="ltx_ref">24</a>, <a href="#bib.bib31" title="" class="ltx_ref">31</a>, <a href="#bib.bib11" title="" class="ltx_ref">11</a>, <a href="#bib.bib30" title="" class="ltx_ref">30</a>]</cite>.
Here, the similarity of two clusters is the average edge weight between them (non-present edges are treated as having weight <math id="S1.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>0</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">0</annotation></semantics></math>). In other words, average linkage HAC repeatedly merges the two clusters with the highest average edge weight between them (see <a href="#S1.F1" title="In 1 Introduction ‣ It’s Hard to HAC with Average Linkage!" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> for an example).</p>
</div>
<figure id="S1.F1" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_3">
<figure id="S1.F1.sf1" class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_align_center"><object type="image/svg+xml" data="/html/2404.14730/assets/hac1.svg" id="S1.F1.sf1.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_portrait" width="476" height="938"></object>

</figure></div><div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_3">

<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S1.F1.sf3.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">(c)</span> </span><span id="S1.F1.sf3.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Dendrogram.</span></figcaption>
</figure></div></div>
<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S1.F1.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 1</span>: </span><span id="S1.F1.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">An example of average linkage HAC run on an input graph <math id="S1.F1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math>. Edges labeled with weights. <a href="#S1.F1.sf1" title="Figure 1(a) ‣ Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ It’s Hard to HAC with Average Linkage!" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1(a)</span></a> gives <math id="S1.F1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math>. <a href="#S1.F1.sf2" title="Figure 1(b) ‣ Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ It’s Hard to HAC with Average Linkage!" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1(b)</span></a> gives the cluster hierarchy output by HAC. <a href="#S1.F1.sf3" title="Figure 1(c) ‣ Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ It’s Hard to HAC with Average Linkage!" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1(c)</span></a> gives the corresponding dendrogram with internal nodes labeled with the weight of their corresponding merge.</span></figcaption>
</figure>
<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p4.1" class="ltx_p">A natural algorithmic question then is how quickly can we solve average linkage HAC on <math id="S1.p4.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> node and <math id="S1.p4.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="m" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>m</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m</annotation></semantics></math> edge graphs?
Recent work has provided a partial answer to this basic question in sequential and parallel models of computation.
In particular, <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib15" title="" class="ltx_ref">15</a>]</cite> showed that average linkage HAC can be solved in <math id="S1.p4.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{O}(n\sqrt{m})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>O</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><msqrt><mi>m</mi></msqrt></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{O}(n\sqrt{m})</annotation></semantics></math> time, thus providing a sub-quadratic time algorithm for sufficiently sparse graphs.
A follow-up paper studied average linkage HAC in the parallel setting and showed that the problem is <span id="S1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">P</span>-complete and so likely does not admit <span id="S1.p4.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">NC</span> algorithms <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib16" title="" class="ltx_ref">16</a>]</cite>.
However, the <span id="S1.p4.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">P</span>-completeness result of <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib16" title="" class="ltx_ref">16</a>]</cite> holds for worst case graphs whereas typical applications of HAC are on highly structured graphs—namely those which are meant to capture relevant properties of an underlying metric—and so there is still hope for parallelizing average linkage HAC on more structured instances.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.p5" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.p5.1" class="ltx_p">In fact, such structured instances of average linkage HAC are known to admit much faster algorithms in the sequential setting: the sequential algorithm of <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib15" title="" class="ltx_ref">15</a>]</cite> implies that if the input graph is planar (or, more generally, minor-free) average linkage HAC can be solved in time <math id="S1.p5.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{O}(m)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>O</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{O}(m)</annotation></semantics></math>.
More generally, if each graph obtained by contracting all clusters at each step of average linkage HAC has <math id="S1.p5.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="O(1)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">O(1)</annotation></semantics></math> arboricity<span id="footnote1" class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">1</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup class="ltx_note_mark">1</sup>
            <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">1</span>
            
            
            
            
            
            
            
          A graph has arboricity at most <math id="footnote1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\alpha" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>α</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math> if all of its edges can be covered by at most <math id="footnote1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\alpha" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>α</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha</annotation></semantics></math> trees.</span></span></span>, then it is possible to solve average linkage HAC in time <math id="S1.p5.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{O}(m)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>O</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{O}(m)</annotation></semantics></math>; it follows that average linkage HAC can be solved in sequential time <math id="S1.p5.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{O}(m)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>O</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{O}(m)</annotation></semantics></math> on trees or planar graphs.
In light of these improved sequential results for highly structured graphs, it becomes natural to hope for efficient parallel algorithms on structured graphs such as low arboricity graphs or, even, just trees.</p>
</div>
<section id="S1.SS1" class="ltx_subsection">
<h3 class="ltx_title ltx_title_subsection"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">1.1 </span>Our Contributions</h3>

<div id="S1.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS1.p1.1" class="ltx_p">In this work, we continue the line of work which studied the computational complexity of different variants of HAC <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib20" title="" class="ltx_ref">20</a>, <a href="#bib.bib40" title="" class="ltx_ref">40</a>, <a href="#bib.bib1" title="" class="ltx_ref">1</a>, <a href="#bib.bib15" title="" class="ltx_ref">15</a>, <a href="#bib.bib16" title="" class="ltx_ref">16</a>]</cite> and perform a careful investigation into the complexity of average linkage HAC.
In particular, we study HAC on <math id="S1.SS1.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics></math> node and <math id="S1.SS1.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="m" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>m</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">m</annotation></semantics></math> edge graphs and investigate whether
near-linear time algorithms, or more efficient parallel algorithms are possible, namely:</p>
<ol id="S1.I1" class="ltx_enumerate">
<li id="S1.I1.i1" class="ltx_item" style="list-style-type:none;"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">1.</span> 
<div id="S1.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.I1.i1.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S1.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Near-Linear Time Algorithms:</span> Can we improve over the best known <math id="S1.I1.i1.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{O}(n\sqrt{m})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>O</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><msqrt><mi>m</mi></msqrt></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{O}(n\sqrt{m})</annotation></semantics></math> upper bound for average linkage HAC and obtain near-linear time sequential algorithms?</p>
</div></li>
<li id="S1.I1.i2" class="ltx_item" style="list-style-type:none;"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">2.</span> 
<div id="S1.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.I1.i2.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="S1.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">NC<span id="S1.I1.i2.p1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_upright"> Algorithms:</span></span> are there <math id="S1.I1.i2.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="{\mathsf{polylog}}(n)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒𝗅𝗈𝗀</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">{\mathsf{polylog}}(n)</annotation></semantics></math> depth parallel algorithms for average linkage HAC with <math id="S1.I1.i2.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{poly}(n)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>𝗉𝗈𝗅𝗒</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{poly}(n)</annotation></semantics></math> work for highly structured instances, e.g., trees, or minor-closed graphs?</p>
</div></li>
</ol>
<p id="S1.SS1.p1.2" class="ltx_p">We give both new lower bounds which (conditionally) rule out near-linear time and <span id="S1.SS1.p1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">NC</span> algorithms, and provide conditions under which these impossibility results can be bypassed.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS1.p2.1" class="ltx_p">First, we demonstrate that near-linear time algorithms are impossible under standard fine-grained complexity assumptions.</p>
</div>
<div id="Thmtheorem1" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_theorem">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_title_theorem"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="Thmtheorem1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Theorem 1</span></span><span id="Thmtheorem1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span></h6>
<div id="Thmtheorem1.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="Thmtheorem1.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="Thmtheorem1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">If average linkage HAC can be solved by a combinatorial algorithm in <math id="Thmtheorem1.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="O(n^{3/2-\epsilon})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>n</mi><mrow><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>/</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><mo>−</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">O(n^{3/2-\epsilon})</annotation></semantics></math> time for any <math id="Thmtheorem1.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon&gt;0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon&gt;0</annotation></semantics></math>, then the Combinatorial Boolean Matrix Multiplication (Combinatorial BMM) Conjecture is false.</span></p>
</div>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS1.p3.1" class="ltx_p">Our reduction also implies a second (weaker) conditional lower bound that also holds for non-combinatorial algorithms (e.g., algebraic algorithms) based on the running time of matrix multiplication.
In particular, for two <math id="S1.SS1.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="n\times n" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo lspace="0.222em" rspace="0.222em">×</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">n\times n</annotation></semantics></math> binary matrices, it is well known that matrix multiplication can be solved in time <math id="S1.SS1.p3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="O(n^{\omega})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>n</mi><mi>ω</mi></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">O(n^{\omega})</annotation></semantics></math> where <math id="S1.SS1.p3.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="2\leq\omega&lt;2.3716" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>≤</mo><mi>ω</mi><mo>&lt;</mo><mn>2.3716</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">2\leq\omega&lt;2.3716</annotation></semantics></math> <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib44" title="" class="ltx_ref">44</a>]</cite>.
In this setting, we obtain the following result:</p>
</div>
<div id="Thmtheorem2" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_theorem">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_title_theorem"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="Thmtheorem2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Theorem 2</span></span><span id="Thmtheorem2.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span></h6>
<div id="Thmtheorem2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="Thmtheorem2.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="Thmtheorem2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">If average linkage HAC can be solved by an algorithm in <math id="Thmtheorem2.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="O(n^{\omega/2-\epsilon})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>n</mi><mrow><mrow><mi>ω</mi><mo>/</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow><mo>−</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">O(n^{\omega/2-\epsilon})</annotation></semantics></math> time for some <math id="Thmtheorem2.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon&gt;0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon&gt;0</annotation></semantics></math>, then boolean matrix multiplication can be solved in <math id="Thmtheorem2.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="O(n^{\omega-\epsilon^{\prime}})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>n</mi><mrow><mi>ω</mi><mo>−</mo><msup><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>′</mo></msup></mrow></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">O(n^{\omega-\epsilon^{\prime}})</annotation></semantics></math> time for some <math id="Thmtheorem2.p1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon^{\prime}&gt;0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msup><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>′</mo></msup><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon^{\prime}&gt;0</annotation></semantics></math>.</span></p>
</div>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S1.SS1.p4.1" class="ltx_p">Notably, Theorem <a href="#Thmtheorem1" title="Theorem 1. ‣ 1.1 Our Contributions ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ It’s Hard to HAC with Average Linkage!" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> shows that the prior running time of <math id="S1.SS1.p4.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\tilde{O}(n\sqrt{m})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mover accent="true"><mi>O</mi><mo>~</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><msqrt><mi>m</mi></msqrt></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\tilde{O}(n\sqrt{m})</annotation></semantics></math> of <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib15" title="" class="ltx_ref">15</a>]</cite> is <span id="S1.SS1.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">optimal</span> up to logarithmic factors under standard fine-grained complexity assumptions, at least for graphs consisting of <math id="S1.SS1.p4.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="O(n)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">O(n)</annotation></semantics></math> many edges.
We obtain this conditional lower bound by showing that a carefully constructed instance of HAC can be used to solve the triangle detection problem, which is sub-cubically equivalent to Boolean Matrix Multiplication <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib43" title="" class="ltx_ref">43</a>]</cite>.
We obtain a bound of (essentially) <math id="S1.SS1.p4.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\Omega(n^{3/2})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Ω</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>n</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>/</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\Omega(n^{3/2})</annotation></semantics></math> since our reduction incurs a quadratic time and space blowup when transforming an input triangle detection instance to an instance of average linkage HAC.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS1.p5" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS1.p5.1" class="ltx_p">We next turn to the parallel setting.
Here, we show that HAC—<em id="S1.SS1.p5.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">even on trees</em>—is unlikely to admit efficient parallel algorithms.
More formally, we show that average linkage HAC on low diameter trees is as hard as any problem in the complexity class Comparator Circuit (<span id="S1.SS1.p5.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">CC</span>) <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib39" title="" class="ltx_ref">39</a>, <a href="#bib.bib12" title="" class="ltx_ref">12</a>]</cite>.
It is believed that <span id="S1.SS1.p5.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">CC</span> is incomparable with <span id="S1.SS1.p5.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">NC</span> and that <span id="S1.SS1.p5.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">CC</span>-hardness is evidence that a problem is not parallelizable <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib29" title="" class="ltx_ref">29</a>, <a href="#bib.bib12" title="" class="ltx_ref">12</a>]</cite>.</p>
</div>
<div id="Thmtheorem3" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_theorem">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_title_theorem"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="Thmtheorem3.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Theorem 3</span></span><span id="Thmtheorem3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span></h6>
<div id="Thmtheorem3.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="Thmtheorem3.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="Thmtheorem3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Average linkage HAC is <span id="Thmtheorem3.p1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">CC</span>-hard, even on trees of diameter <math id="Thmtheorem3.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="4" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>4</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">4</annotation></semantics></math>.</span></p>
</div>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS1.p6" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S1.SS1.p6.1" class="ltx_p">We note that it is known that <math id="S1.SS1.p6.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\textsc{CC}\subseteq\textsc{P}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mtext class="ltx_font_smallcaps">CC</mtext><mo>⊆</mo><mtext class="ltx_font_smallcaps">P</mtext></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\textsc{CC}\subseteq\textsc{P}</annotation></semantics></math> and so the <span id="S1.SS1.p6.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_markedasmath ltx_font_smallcaps">P</span>-hardness of <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib16" title="" class="ltx_ref">16</a>]</cite> already suggests the impossibility of efficient parallel algorithms on <em id="S1.SS1.p6.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">general graphs</em>.
However, our result suggests the impossibility of efficient parallel algorithms <em id="S1.SS1.p6.1.3" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">even on very simple graphs</em> (trees of diameter 4).
We obtain this result by reducing from the lexicographically first maximal matching (<span id="S1.SS1.p6.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_sansserif">LFM Matching</span>) problem and an intermediate problem which we call <span id="S1.SS1.p6.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_sansserif">Adaptive Minimum</span>, which captures some of what makes HAC intrinsically difficult to parallelize.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS1.p7" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS1.p7.1" class="ltx_p">On the positive side, we demonstrate that average linkage HAC on path graphs is in <span id="S1.SS1.p7.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">NC</span>, under the mild assumption that the aspect ratio is polynomial.
While the class of path graphs is restrictive, even on paths average linkage is highly non-trivial and naively running HAC requires resolving chains of <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\Omega(n)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Ω</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\Omega(n)</annotation></semantics></math> sequential dependencies. For example, consider a path of vertices <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{n})" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{n})</annotation></semantics></math> where the edge <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\{v_{i},v_{i+1}\}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\{v_{i},v_{i+1}\}</annotation></semantics></math> has weight <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="1+i\cdot\epsilon" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo lspace="0.222em" rspace="0.222em">⋅</mo><mi>ϵ</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1+i\cdot\epsilon</annotation></semantics></math> for some small <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\epsilon&gt;0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>ϵ</mi><mo>&gt;</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\epsilon&gt;0</annotation></semantics></math> and initially each vertex is in its own cluster. Initially, <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v_{n}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>v</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v_{n}</annotation></semantics></math>’s most similar neighbor is <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m7" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v_{n-1}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>v</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v_{n-1}</annotation></semantics></math> and so <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m8" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v_{n}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>v</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v_{n}</annotation></semantics></math> would like to merge with <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m9" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v_{n-1}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>v</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v_{n-1}</annotation></semantics></math> but <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m10" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v_{n-1}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>v</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v_{n-1}</annotation></semantics></math>’s most similar neighbor is <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m11" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v_{n-2}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>v</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v_{n-2}</annotation></semantics></math> and so on. Thus, whether or not <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m12" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v_{n}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>v</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v_{n}</annotation></semantics></math> gets to merge with <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m13" class="ltx_Math" alttext="v_{n-1}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>v</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">v_{n-1}</annotation></semantics></math> depends on the merge behavior of <math id="S1.SS1.p7.m14" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\Theta(n)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Θ</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\Theta(n)</annotation></semantics></math> other clusters and so it is not at all clear that <span id="S1.SS1.p7.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">NC</span> algorithms should be possible for this setting. Nonetheless, we show the following.</p>
</div>
<div id="Thmtheorem4" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_theorem">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_title_theorem"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="Thmtheorem4.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Theorem 4</span></span><span id="Thmtheorem4.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span></h6>
<div id="Thmtheorem4.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="Thmtheorem4.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="Thmtheorem4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Average linkage HAC on paths is in <span id="Thmtheorem4.p1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">NC</span>. In particular, there is an algorithm for average linkage HAC that runs in <math id="Thmtheorem4.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="O(\log^{2}n\log\log n)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mrow><msup><mi>log</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow><mo lspace="0.167em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">O(\log^{2}n\log\log n)</annotation></semantics></math> depth with <math id="Thmtheorem4.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="O(n\log n\log\log n)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo lspace="0.167em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow><mo lspace="0.167em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>log</mi></mrow><mo lspace="0.167em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">O(n\log n\log\log n)</annotation></semantics></math> work.</span></p>
</div>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS1.p8" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S1.SS1.p8.1" class="ltx_p">The above algorithm leverages the fact that in average linkage HAC the maximum edge similarity monotonically decreases. In particular, it works in <math id="S1.SS1.p8.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="O(\log n)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">O(\log n)</annotation></semantics></math> phases where each phase consists of merges of equal similarity up to constants. The goal then becomes to efficiently perform merges until every edge is no longer within a constant of the starting maximum similarity of the phase. The starting point of the algorithm is to observe that <math id="S1.SS1.p8.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\Omega(n)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">Ω</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\Omega(n)</annotation></semantics></math> sequential dependencies of clusters of equal size can be resolved efficiently in parallel in a phase by noting that in this phase only the odd-indexed edges merge in the chain. Thus, each edge can decide if it is odd-indexed in parallel by, e.g., using <math id="S1.SS1.p8.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\mathsf{prefix\text{-}sum}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>𝗉𝗋𝖾𝖿𝗂𝗑</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mtext>-</mtext><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>𝗌𝗎𝗆</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\mathsf{prefix\text{-}sum}</annotation></semantics></math>, which is well known to be solvable in linear work in <span id="S1.SS1.p8.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">NC</span>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS1.p9" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS1.p9.1" class="ltx_p">For chains with clusters of general weights, we decompose dependency chains into short (<math id="S1.SS1.p9.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="(O(\log n)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo fence="true" lspace="0em">CLOSE</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">(O(\log n)</annotation></semantics></math>-length) subchains where resolving dependencies within the subchain must be done sequentially but in the current phase each subchain’s merge behavior only depends on whether or not its closest neighboring subchains merges into it or not. Thus, each subchain can compute its merge behavior for these two cases and then, similar to the equal weights setting, we propagate merge behavior across subchains efficiently in parallel.</p>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS1.p10" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S1.SS1.p10.1" class="ltx_p">To complement our sequential lower bound with a positive result, we demonstrate that it is possible to achieve near-linear running time, provided the dendrogram has low height. Thus, if the output dendrogram is a relatively balanced tree, then near-linear time algorithms are possible.</p>
</div>
<div id="Thmtheorem5" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_theorem">
<h6 class="ltx_title ltx_runin ltx_title_theorem"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="Thmtheorem5.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Theorem 5</span></span><span id="Thmtheorem5.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span></h6>
<div id="Thmtheorem5.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="Thmtheorem5.p1.1" class="ltx_p"><span id="Thmtheorem5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">There is an implementation of the nearest-neighbor chain algorithm for average linkage HAC that runs in <math id="Thmtheorem5.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="O(m\cdot h\log n)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>O</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo lspace="0.222em" rspace="0.222em">⋅</mo><mi>h</mi></mrow><mo lspace="0.167em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">O(m\cdot h\log n)</annotation></semantics></math> time where <math id="Thmtheorem5.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="h" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>h</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">h</annotation></semantics></math> is the height of the output dendrogram.</span></p>
</div>
</div>
<div id="S1.SS1.p11" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
<p id="S1.SS1.p11.1" class="ltx_p">The above result is in fact obtained by a relatively simple (but to the best of our knowledge, new) analysis of <span id="S1.SS1.p11.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">existing</span> classic HAC algorithms.
In particular, we show that the nearest-neighbor chain <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib6" title="" class="ltx_ref">6</a>, <a href="#bib.bib22" title="" class="ltx_ref">22</a>]</cite> and heap-based algorithms <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib28" title="" class="ltx_ref">28</a>]</cite> for HAC, which were developed over 40 years ago achieve this bound.</p>
</div>
</section>
</section>
<section id="S2" class="ltx_section">
<h2 class="ltx_title ltx_title_section"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>Preliminaries</h2>

<div id="S2.p1" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p1.1" class="ltx_p">The input to the HAC algorithm is an undirected weighted graph <math id="S2.p1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G=(V,E,w)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>G</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>V</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>E</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>w</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G=(V,E,w)</annotation></semantics></math>, where <math id="S2.p1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w:V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{0\}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo lspace="0.278em" rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>V</mi><mo lspace="0.222em" rspace="0.222em">×</mo><mi>V</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="false">→</mo><mrow><msub><mi>ℝ</mi><mo>+</mo></msub><mo>∪</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mn>0</mn><mo stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w:V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{0\}</annotation></semantics></math> is a function assigning nonnegative weights to the edges.
For convenience we assume <math id="S2.p1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(x,y)=0" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(x,y)=0</annotation></semantics></math> when <math id="S2.p1.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="xy\not\in E" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow><mo>∉</mo><mi>E</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">xy\not\in E</annotation></semantics></math>.
The vanilla version of average linkage HAC is given as <a href="#alg1" title="In 2 Preliminaries ‣ It’s Hard to HAC with Average Linkage!" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Algorithm</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>.
It starts by putting each vertex in a cluster of size <math id="S2.p1.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="1" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mn>1</mn><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">1</annotation></semantics></math> and then repeats the following step.
While there is a pair of clusters of positive similarity, find two most similar clusters and merge them together, that is, replace them by their union.
The similarity between two clusters is the total edge weight between them divided by the product of the cluster sizes.
We refer to this version as the <em id="S2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">static graph</em> version, since the graph is not changed throughout the run of the algorithm.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.p2" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p2.1" class="ltx_p">Throughout the paper we usually work with a different (equivalent) way of presenting the same algorithm which is given as <a href="#alg2" title="In 2 Preliminaries ‣ It’s Hard to HAC with Average Linkage!" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Algorithm</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> (e.g., <a href="#alg4" title="In Height of the dendrogram is 
          
            
              O
              
                
                
                  (
                  
                    
                      
                      log
                      2
                    
                    n
                  
                  ) ‣ 5 Average Linkage HAC on Paths in NC ‣ It’s Hard to HAC with Average Linkage!" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Algorithms</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> and <a href="#alg5" title="Algorithm 5 ‣ Nearest-Neighbor Chain Analysis ‣ 6 
        
          
            O
            
              
              
                (
                
                  ⁢
                  m
                  h
                  
                    log
                    n
                  
                
                )
              
            
          
        
       Upper Bound ‣ It’s Hard to HAC with Average Linkage!" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a>).
In this version we maintain a graph <math id="S2.p2.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math> whose vertices are clusters.
The <em id="S2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">size</em> of the vertex is the size of the cluster it represents.
The <em id="S2.p2.1.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">normalized</em> weight of an edge <math id="S2.p2.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="xy" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>y</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">xy</annotation></semantics></math> in <math id="S2.p2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math> is <math id="S2.p2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="w(x,y)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">w(x,y)</annotation></semantics></math> divided by the product of the sizes of <math id="S2.p2.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>x</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S2.p2.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="y" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>y</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">y</annotation></semantics></math>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.p3" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p3.1" class="ltx_p">Whenever two clusters merge, their corresponding vertices are merged into one, i.e., the edge between them is contracted and the size of the new vertex is the sum of the sizes of the vertices that merged.
In the following we sometimes say that a vertex <math id="S2.p3.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>x</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math> merges into vertex <math id="S2.p3.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="y" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>y</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">y</annotation></semantics></math>.
In this case we simply assume that the name of the resulting vertex is <math id="S2.p3.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="y" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>y</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">y</annotation></semantics></math> and the size of <math id="S2.p3.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="y" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>y</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">y</annotation></semantics></math> is increased by the size of <math id="S2.p3.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="x" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>x</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">x</annotation></semantics></math>. See <a href="#S2.F2" title="In 2 Preliminaries ‣ It’s Hard to HAC with Average Linkage!" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>.</p>
</div>

<figcaption class="ltx_caption ltx_centering"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S2.F2.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 2</span>: </span><span id="S2.F2.4" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">An example of average linkage HAC run on an input graph <math id="S2.F2.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math> where we imagine we contract merged clusters. Intermediate vertices labeled with the vertices of <math id="S2.F2.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics></math> their corresponding cluster contains. Edges labeled with their weight and next merged edge is dashed. </span></figcaption>
</figure>
<div id="S2.p4" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p4.1" class="ltx_p">The output of HAC is a <em id="S2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_italic">dendrogram</em>—a rooted binary tree representing the cluster merges performed by the algorithm.
Every node of the dendrogram is a cluster built by the algorithm.
There are exactly <math id="S2.p4.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="|V|" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>V</mi><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">|V|</annotation></semantics></math> leaves corresponding to the single-element clusters that are formed in the beginning of the algorithm.
Whenever two clusters <math id="S2.p4.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C_{1}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C_{1}</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S2.p4.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C_{2}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C_{2}</annotation></semantics></math> are merged, we add to the dendrogram a new node <math id="S2.p4.m4" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C_{1}\cup C_{2}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>∪</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C_{1}\cup C_{2}</annotation></semantics></math> whose children are <math id="S2.p4.m5" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C_{1}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C_{1}</annotation></semantics></math> and <math id="S2.p4.m6" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C_{2}" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C_{2}</annotation></semantics></math>. See <a href="#S1.F1.sf3" title="In Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ It’s Hard to HAC with Average Linkage!" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1(c)</span></a> for the dendrogram of <a href="#S2.F2" title="In 2 Preliminaries ‣ It’s Hard to HAC with Average Linkage!" class="ltx_ref"><span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>.</p>
</div>
<div id="S2.p5" class="ltx_para">
<p id="S2.p5.1" class="ltx_p">We use the classic multithreaded model <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib9" title="" class="ltx_ref">9</a>, <a href="#bib.bib4" title="" class="ltx_ref">4</a>, <a href="#bib.bib8" title="" class="ltx_ref">8</a>]</cite> (formally, the MP-RAM <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib8" title="" class="ltx_ref">8</a>]</cite>) to analyze the parallel algorithms.
We assume a set of threads that share the memory.
Each thread acts like a sequential RAM plus a <span id="S2.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">fork</span> instruction that forks two new child threads.
When a thread performs a fork, the two child threads can both start by running their next instructions, and the original thread is suspended until both children terminate.
A computation starts with a single root thread and finishes when that root thread finishes.
A parallel for-loop can be viewed as executing <span id="S2.p5.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">fork</span>s for a logarithmic number of levels.
A computation can thus be viewed as a DAG (directed acyclic graph).
We say the <span id="S2.p5.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">work</span> is the total number of operations in this DAG and <span id="S2.p5.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">span (depth)</span> is equal to the longest path in the DAG.
We note that computations in this model can be cross-simulated in standard variants of the PRAM model in the same work (asymptotically), and losing at most a single logarithmic factor in the depth <cite class="ltx_cite ltx_citemacro_cite">[<a href="#bib.bib8" title="" class="ltx_ref">8</a>]</cite>.</p>
</div>
<figure id="alg1" class="ltx_float ltx_algorithm">
<div id="alg1.3" class="ltx_listing ltx_lst_numbers_left ltx_listing">
<div id="alg1.3.1" class="ltx_listingline">
<span id="alg1.3.1.1" class="ltx_text"><span id="alg1.3.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Input:</span> </span><math id="alg1.m1" class="ltx_Math" alttext="G=(V,E,w)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mi>G</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>V</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>E</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>w</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">G=(V,E,w)</annotation></semantics></math>
</div>
<div id="alg1.3.2" class="ltx_listingline">




            <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline">1</span>
          
</div>
<div id="alg1.3.3" class="ltx_listingline"><span id="alg1.3.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Function</span> <em id="alg1.3.3.2" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_typewriter">Similarity(<em id="alg1.3.3.2.1" class="ltx_emph ltx_font_serif ltx_font_italic"><math id="alg1.m2" class="ltx_Math" alttext="C_{1},C_{2},w" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi>w</mi></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">C_{1},C_{2},w</annotation></semantics></math></em>)</em><span id="alg1.3.3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">:</span>
            <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline">2</span>
          
</div>
<div id="alg1.3.4" class="ltx_listingline">
<span id="alg1.3.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">return</span> <math id="alg1.m3" class="ltx_Math" alttext="\sum_{x\in C_{1},y\in C_{2}}w(x,y)/(|C_{1}|\cdot|C_{2}|)" display="inline" intent=":literal"><semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>∈</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow><mo>,</mo><mrow><mi>y</mi><mo>∈</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></msub><mrow><mi>w</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>y</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo>/</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo rspace="0.055em" stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo rspace="0.222em">⋅</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\sum_{x\in C_{1},y\in C_{2}}w(x,y)/(|C_{1}|\cdot|C_{2}|)</annotation></semantics></math>

            <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline">3</span>
          
</div>

            <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline">5</span>
